  relay credentials billed to the account, and the worker's `calls_turn` switch cannot stop a
  holder who calls Cloudflare directly. `TEST_HASHES`, `ADMIN_HASHES` and `HIDDEN_HASHES` need no
  rotation (hashes, the latter two already public in `wrangler.jsonc`), and the Terraform R2 pair
  was never in the worker env.

- **The shell is an ES module: the port's frame, half of P0 (2026-09-17)** — the write-path port
  (`docs/architecture/reviews/2026-09-17-port-plan.md`) begins by giving `app/` the seam
  `client/` already has, because its first invariant is that `docs/app.js` never grows: app.js
  rides EVERY page, readers included, so code moved in from the classic client would make a KJV
  chapter heavier unless it can land in a chunk instead. So `app/shell.ts` now builds the way
  `client/comments.ts` has since 2026-09-16 — `--format=esm --splitting --outdir=docs
  --entry-names=app --chunk-names=chunks/[name]-[hash]` — and `docs/nav.js` injects it with
  `s.type = 'module'`. With no dynamic import in the shell yet the output is still one file, 24
  bytes smaller than the IIFE build and byte-deterministic across a double `make bundle`; the
  point is the seam, not today's bytes. Two things that had to agree and now have a test
  (`tests/js/bundle_budget`): an ESM bundle injected as a classic script dies at its first static
  import, and the `rm -rf docs/chunks` that used to open the client build moved ahead of BOTH
  builds — left where it was, the client's build would have deleted the shell's chunks the first
  time the shell had any. Verified locally against `make serve` with headless Chromium before it
  shipped: on index, community and messages the shell reports ready, the kernel and the API stand
  on `window`, all THIRTEEN views register, the classic client still boots on the app pages, the
  board index renders its categories, and a soft hop to community.html and back stays soft (the
  page object survives). A dynamically inserted script is async whether classic or module, and
  what `comments.js` waits on is the `__mcShellReady` flag and the `mc-shell-ready` event, not
  load order — which is why the format change is invisible to it.

- **The worker's handlers take the real `Env` (2026-09-17)** — `comments-worker/src/env.ts` had
  described every binding since 2026-09-16 and said the handlers would adopt it one file at a time;
  they now have, all of them. Fourteen modules in four commits: the nine small ones (analytics, db,
  quota, usage, index's config handler, the ChatRoom, and the notify/media/profile routes), then
  admin, then board, dm and merecat, then `lib.ts` — 100 `env: any` in that file alone. Handlers
  read `(request: Request, env: Env, ctx: ExecutionContext, url: URL)`; a body is `Body` (an open
  record of unknowns, every field still coerced exactly as before); a D1 read carries its row shape
  at the query (`first<T>()` / `all<T>()`), so a count is a number and `page` is a string rather
  than `{}`. The worker's `: any` count fell **799 → 388** across the day, each fall recorded in
  `tests/_support/any_baseline.json`. **It paid for itself on the first file**: typing
  `handleRecent`'s env is what surfaced the six-week secret disclosure above. Four smaller things
  the compiler also found, each fixed rather than silenced: the ChatRoom's `#hello` served a
  `backend` field deleted when the GPU backend was retired; the avatar and DM uploads guarded
  `typeof file.arrayBuffer !== 'function'` where a FormData value is a string or a File; `keyed`
  took `bucket: string` and looked it up on env (it is one of three named limiters, and says so
  now); `verifyTurnstile` handed the optional secret straight to `URLSearchParams`. Seven source
  locks moved with the code — each re-anchored on the RULE rather than the annotation (the
  allowlist by `const allowed`, the board edit's SELECT accepting `.first()` or `.first<Row>()`,
  merecatPrompt's level as the last parameter defaulting to off, the journal predicates by name,
  the generation object under either spelling) — which is the cost the 09-16 review predicted for
  text locks, paid once and written down. What a type could not reach stayed honest: four Workers
  AI calls take a union the binding cannot narrow and state the shape the next line reads, and the
  reranker, absent from the generated model catalogue, says so in a comment.

- **A public endpoint served the worker's whole env for six weeks (2026-09-17)** — the Env typing
  pass turned `handleRecent` (`comments-worker/src/routes/wall.ts`) from `env: any` to `env: Env`
  and tsc answered with a mismatch on its last line: `items = await withNames(env, items)` — two
  arguments handed to `db.ts`'s `withNames(row, posts)` mapper. That mapper opens
  `Object.assign({}, row)`, so the row it copied was the **worker env itself**, and
  `GET /api/comments/recent` — public, keyless, `Cache-Control: public, max-age=300` — answered
  with every binding and every secret string the worker holds: TURNSTILE_SECRET,
  VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY, TURN_KEY_SECRET, CF_USAGE_TOKEN, MERECAT_INGEST_KEY, MC_TEST_BYPASS,
  TEST_HASHES, ADMIN_HASHES, HIDDEN_HASHES, CF_ACCOUNT_ID, TURN_KEY_ID and the rest. Verified
  against production before anything was touched: 8,770 bytes, `items` an OBJECT, twenty secret
  values in it. The line was wrong the day it was written (`27fd749`, 2026-08-02) and rode the
  route split unchanged, so the window is **2026-08-02 to 2026-09-17, about six weeks**. Nothing
  was red the whole time: `withNames` took `any`, the handler took `env: any`, the route answered
  200 with a plausible envelope, and no test had ever read a response body for something it
  should NOT contain. Fixed to the enrichment every other list does (`postCountsFor` then
  `withNames(r, counts[...])`), deployed by the emergency road at once (`wrangler deploy`,
  version `afbedc41`), the URL purged from the edge, then committed so CI is the build of record
  again. **Every credential the env named must be treated as disclosed and rotated** — the owner
  was told the same hour with the list. The guard is a SWEEP, `tests/worker/env_leak.test.mjs`:
  every route in the table is called against an env whose vars and secrets are sentinel strings,
  and no answer may contain a sentinel, nor three or more binding NAMES as keys, nor may
  `/recent` stop being a list; a third leg refuses `withNames(env` in any worker source. Proven by
  re-introducing the bug: the sweep fails three ways, and passes once the fix is back. The lesson
  the review had already written and this proves: `any` on a boundary is not a style question —
  it is the difference between a compiler error and six weeks of disclosure.

- **Four ceilings lifted in one change: replicas for the reads, presence only where it is watched,
  limits per member, and a librarian that stops counting its shelf (2026-09-17)** — the scale review
  after the hub was sharded named the next four walls, and the owner asked for all of them at once.
  (1) **D1 read replicas.** Nothing used the Sessions API, so every query went to the one
  single-threaded primary. Every routed request now runs against a session
  (`comments-worker/src/dbsession.ts`): a route on `Domain.Consistency.replicaRoutes` starts
  wherever D1 likes, or from the browser's bookmark; every other route starts at the primary. The
  list is an allowlist on purpose — several write handlers read before they write (the DM media
  reference count before a purge, the roster a sealed message must equal, the live media budget),
  and a stale replica read there would be wrong, not slow. `/dm/thread` was on the first draft and
  came off in review: opening a conversation starts the disappearing clock from the lifetime it just
  read. `tests/worker/d1_session.test.mjs` refuses any listed handler that writes. A request that
  wrote (any statement the kernel's `isWriteSql` does not call a read) answers with an HttpOnly
  `mc-d1` bookmark cookie on `/api`, so the member's next read is at least as fresh as their own
  write, with no client code (the API is same-origin). The adapter keeps the binding's shape; crons
  and both Durable Objects keep the primary. `terraform/d1.tf` turns `read_replication` to `auto` on
  the comments database alone (planned locally first: an in-place update; the CI apply then failed
because the Terraform token holds D1 Read only, and the owner had it applied once with the workers
token — CICD §10, exception 13), and the ops probe carries
  `d1.served_by_primary` / `served_by_region` from one unconstrained read. (2) **Targeted
  presence.** Every presence change had been relayed to every sibling shard, so each shard received
  every login on the site — a ceiling of roughly a thousand changes a second that no shard count
  could lift. A member's home shard now keeps, in the BoardHub's own SQLite (a `watch` table,
  untouched when `HUB_SHARDS` is 1), which siblings hold a watcher: a sibling registers through the
  new `watch` RPC in the same turn that answers the presence seed, a change is relayed to the
  registered shards alone, and a sibling that finds nobody watching answers `idle` so the row goes —
  the delete is guarded by the row's registration stamp, so a watcher that returns while that answer
  is in flight is not lost (a test drives exactly that interleaving). A member whose socket sits off
  its home shard still relays to every sibling; rows written under another shard count are dropped
  on wake. Storage does not keep an object from hibernating (Cloudflare's lifecycle page names
  timers, pending I/O, the standard WebSocket API and outbound sockets). (3) **Rate limits per
  member.** Every limit keyed on the address, so a parish on one Wi-Fi shared a single 120-a-minute
  read allowance. `lib.ts` `throttle` is now the only caller of `.limit(` (the test sweeps the
  worker for any other): a request that names an identity counts against that member's bucket
  (`Domain.Throttle.memberBucket`, never the empty key's hash), and every request also counts
  against a per-address backstop — `READ_IP_LIMIT` 1,200, `POST_IP_LIMIT` 300, `CONNECT_IP_LIMIT`
  300 a minute — because a key is only claimed there: rotating keys escapes a member bucket, never
  the backstop. Uploads, whose key sits inside a multipart body, count at the address alone; the
  socket upgrade counts against the hinted member. The member numbers are unchanged, so the client's
  `READ_CEIL` stays 120. (4) **The librarian's sizes.** The pipeline's size projection summed
  `LENGTH(text)` over every chunk of every room on each run (the deep rooms read 49,076 and 25,046
  rows a call, up to 2.5 s at p90, three million rows on 2026-09-11 against the free five million).
  `works.text_bytes` is now stamped at ingest end from the work's own chunks, `/works` sums the
  column and the admin stats sum the stamped chunk counts; the rooms have no migration ledger, so
  `ensureLibSchema` adds the column once per isolate per room and backfills only the works that have
  chunks and no size, and `tests/worker/librarian_sizes.test.mjs` proves the backfilled totals equal
  the old scan's. Not done: the writes a keyed read still makes (`registerMember` on the prefs and
  board-unread roads) go to the primary as before.

- **Nothing the worker holds in secret leaves it: the env is sealed and every answer is scanned
  (2026-09-17)** — the disclosure's remedy as two walls at the places every answer already passes,
  so the class is closed whoever writes the next handler. `comments-worker/src/egress.ts`:
  `sealEnv` wraps the env in a Proxy over an EMPTY target (so no Proxy invariant ties it to the
  runtime's property attributes) that forwards reads by name and refuses the rest — `ownKeys`,
  `toJSON`, writes and deletes throw `EnvLeak` — so `Object.assign({}, env)`, a spread, a rest
  pattern, `Object.keys`, `for…in` and a `JSON.stringify` of anything holding the env all fail
  instead of copying; `withNames(env, [])` now throws, which is the bug itself. A seal is made per
  request and remembers each refusal, so a handler whose own `try/catch` swallows the throw is still
  reported. `deriveEnv` keeps the seal when dbsession.ts swaps `DB` for a D1 session (its
  `Object.create(env)` would otherwise have turned a copy of the routed env into a silent `{DB}`).
  `guardResponse` reads every TEXTUAL answer (text, JSON, XML, JavaScript types; never an image, a
  media stream, a ciphertext or a typeless body) and replaces one that carries the value of any
  env string that is not a declared public var with the usual 500. Default-deny: `env.ts`
  `PUBLIC_VARS` names the vars (held to the Env's vars section and to wrangler.jsonc by
  `tests/worker/egress.test.mjs`), everything else of 16 characters or more is scanned, so a new
  `wrangler secret put` needs no line anywhere; `TEST_HASHES` (a list of hashes, which appear in
  answers by design) stayed unscanned only until the Turnstile test bypass was retired (below).
  `serve.ts` is now `default.fetch`: seal, route, guard; the crons seal too, and both Durable
  Objects seal in their constructors and send every frame through one checked `#send` (the
  BoardHub memoises the last clean payload, since a fan-out repeats it). A refusal logs
  `egress_blocked` (a trip `env_enumerated`) with the secret's NAME only, and `ops.ts` `noteLeak`
  keeps an `ops_egress` tally row: told at once through the Alerts channels, at most hourly while
  it continues (`Domain.Ops.shouldRetell`), an isolate writing a key at most once a minute; any note
  younger than a day turns the health verdict red (`leakStanding`), so ops-watch fails too, and the
  Health card has a line for it. The self-check names a secret too short to scan (`secret_short`).
  contact-worker imports the same module (its own public set, `contact-worker/src/vars.ts`; its
  tsconfig gains `allowImportingTsExtensions`). And `Domain.Cache.schema` went to 2, so every
  member's persisted read cache — which held copies of the leaked `/recent` answers — is dropped on
  their next visit. The cost is one text scan per answer, well inside a free invocation's CPU.

- **The category and page feeds answered 500 whenever they held a reply (2026-09-17)** — found by
  the leak sweep on its first run with query modes, confirmed on production before the fix:
  `/api/comments/feed?cat=pub` answered `500 Server hiccup` while `?cat=news` (topics only) and
  `?topic=219` worked. `handleFeed` gave a reply (no title) the item title `name + ' on ' +
  pageHref`, and `const pageHref` was declared after the `items` map — a temporal-dead-zone
  `ReferenceError` for any category or page feed whose last fifty posts held a reply, the Discord
  hooks' own feeds included. The declaration moved above the map. The sweep now asks each public
  read in each of its modes alone (`GET_MODES`: the feed by category, by page and by topic; a
  journal article; a topic with `find`; search by author and by date; a profile by handle; a
  filtered category), because the table's one call sends every parameter at once and one mode always
  wins; `env_leak.test.mjs` requires every mode to answer 200 and no handler to throw.

- **The Turnstile test bypass is retired, not rotated (2026-09-17)** — `verifyTurnstile` accepted
  `TEST:<MC_TEST_BYPASS>` as a token for the identities in the `TEST_HASHES` secret, so the
  interactive kit could write past a managed challenge its headless browsers cannot solve. The env
  disclosure published both secrets, and the rotation that followed renewed the bypass — but the
  kit had not needed it since the established-identity skip (`turnstile_skip_established`, on by
  default) spares an established member who sends NO token, and alice and bob are established, with
  their hashes already in the public `HIDDEN_HASHES` var. So the branch is gone: a `TEST:` token is
  asked of siteverify like any other (`tests/worker/turnstile.test.mjs` sends the old token with the
  old secrets still in the env and gets 403, then writes with none); `hiddenHashes` reads the var
  alone; `live_kit._write` sends no token and reports BLOCKED if the skip is off;
  `probe_turnstile.py` now proves a fake token, the retired token and a fresh tokenless key are
  refused and an established tokenless write lands (and is deleted); POLICY.md and CICD §4 say so.
  Both secrets were deleted from the worker (`wrangler secret delete`) once the deploy was proven —
  the probe green, and the real old token refused 403 while its secret was still set — and the
  egress scan's exemption for `TEST_HASHES` (one deploy, since the hashes it listed appear in every
  answer naming a test identity's post) went in the next commit. The turnstile test shows why it
  had to: with the hash list left in its env, the guard refused the established write's answer.

- **The pipeline holds no key (2026-09-17)** — `merecat.yml` and `ops-watch.yml` opened the worker's
  pipeline doors with one static secret, `MC_INGEST_KEY` on the runners and `MERECAT_INGEST_KEY` in
  the worker, and the env disclosure published the worker's copy: whoever read `/recent` in those
  six weeks could rewrite the librarian's shelf, its persona and its dials, and read the health
  object. Rotating it would have left the same shape — a worker that holds a pipeline key can lose
  it again — so the owner chose no key at all, and a reviewer for the persona. Each job that calls
  the worker now asks GitHub for its OIDC token (audience `merecatholicity-comments`) and sends it
  as a Bearer header. `comments-worker/src/oidc.ts` checks the RS256 signature against GitHub's
  published key set (WebCrypto; cached an hour per isolate; a token naming an unknown key refetches
  at most once a minute; any failure refuses) and hands the claims to **`Domain.Pipeline`**: the
  repository and its owner by their numeric ids, `refs/heads/main` as a branch, a push, schedule or
  dispatch (never a pull request's event), a GitHub-hosted runner, and the door's own workflow file
  (`job_workflow_ref`), inside the token's life with a minute of skew. Three doors: `ingest`
  (`/works`, `/ingest`) and `config` (`/config`) from `merecat.yml`, `probe` (the ops report door,
  reading only) from `ops-watch.yml`; `config` also requires the job's `environment` to be
  **`librarian-config`**, a Terraform-declared environment whose required reviewer is the owner
  (applied before any workflow named it, since GitHub creates a referenced environment unprotected).
  So `merecat.yml` split: `ingest` pushes the corpus and stamps `last_ingest` (the worker refuses
  anything more from its token); `config-status` compares `persona.md` and `config.yml` with the
  file hashes the server last took (`/works` now answers `config_file_hash` too); `config` runs only
  when one differs (or `config: force`), waits for *Review deployments*, and pushes what changed.
  The dials now travel only when their file changes, as the persona always did, so a dashboard edit
  stands until then. Concurrency moved to the jobs, so a waiting review never holds the daily
  ingest. A bearer that fails is refused even beside a good key; an admin key still opens every door
  (the owner by hand, `make librarian`); the dev box's nightly reports with a new narrow worker
  secret, `OPS_REPORT_KEY` (`MC_OPS_REPORT_KEY` in `ci.env`), which opens the ops door and nothing
  else. `ingest.py`'s `PipelineAuth` asks the runner for the token, masks it, and asks again a
  minute before it lapses. Proven by `tests/worker/pipeline.test.mjs` (a stand-in issuer,
  `tests/_support/github_oidc.mjs`: a forged key under the same kid, a swapped payload, `alg: none`,
  every claim, the key set's hour and refetch), the kernel spec,
  `tests/py/test_pipeline_workflows.py` (no stored key, `id-token: write` only on the four jobs that
  call a door — and Pages' deploy, whose token the policy refuses — no pull request, the reviewer's
  environment) and the sweep's new `pipeline` road. The static key was honoured for the one deploy
  the move took, every use logged as `pipeline_static_key`; the tail showed none but the operator's
  own checks while the first keyless runs went green — merecat.yml's config-status, the reviewed
  config job (approved by `ci_approve.sh` after reading that only the dials' first file hash was
  missing) and the ingest; ops-watch on its token, and its drill red without it — so the shim went
  in the next commit and the key was deleted from the worker, the Actions secrets and `ci.env`. The
  security review of the change found an older hole on the same road: the dispatch input `only` was
  spliced unquoted into the ingest's command line, so anyone who could dispatch the workflow (the
  private shelf's dispatch token included) could append an `--api` of their own and be handed the
  credential — the static key then, a live token now. It is one validated argument now (work ids and
  commas), and the workflow test refuses any input spliced into a script.

- **The TURN relay switches itself off near the end of its free pool (2026-09-17)** — Realtime
  TURN relays the ~15–20% of calls a direct connection cannot carry, free for the month's first
  1,000 GB of relayed egress and then billed per GB with no cap. `calls_turn` in Platform settings
  was the only brake, and nobody watched the meter at night; the env disclosure (above) also
  published the TURN key, which is spent without the worker at all. The owner chose an automatic
  brake at 95%. `Domain.Call.turnGuardStep` is the rule: with the guard on (`turn_guard_on`,
  default-on — only an explicit no is off, the AI budget guard's polarity) and the relay on, a
  reading at or past `turn_guard_pct` (10–99, default 95) of the pool is a **trip**; a trip
  recorded in an earlier month with the relay still off is a **restore**; a trip recorded while
  the relay is on again (an admin's hand) is **forgotten**, not overruled that night; a guard that
  is off does nothing at all, which is how an admin keeps the relay on past the line, and an
  admin's own switch-off is never the guard's to undo. `usage.ts` `runTurnGuard` joins the 23:30
  usage chain (one analytics select, aggregated by the usage page's own rulebook; no token, no
  read; an unreadable meter fails the step, never trips), writes `calls_turn = 0` with
  `updated_by: turn-guard` and its memory `turn_guard_state`, busts this isolate's settings cache
  (so `/call/turn` answers STUN-only at once), and tells the owner through the Alerts channels
  what happened, how to keep the relay on, and that a spend this high with few calls may be a
  leaked key. `turnGuardRollover` opens the monthly chain and restores silently — nothing to act
  on. Platform settings → Voice calls gains the switch, the line and the guard's last
  switch-off. `tests/worker/turn_guard.test.mjs` RUNS it (the chains, the door's coercion,
  `/call/turn` before and after); the kernel spec is in `call.test.mjs`. The same review fixed
  `scripts/ci_approve.sh`'s traceback after every approval: the endpoint answers an
  environment's name where the printer expected an object.

- **The workers hold no `any`, and the env's type refuses to be an answer (2026-09-17)** — the
  typing pass that found the `/recent` disclosure stopped at 387 `any`s in the worker; the leak's
  cause was exactly one of them (`withNames(env, items)`, both sides `any`), so the rest went too.
  Every handler, gate and helper now takes the type its value has, each tsc complaint checked
  against what the code does before anything changed: the gates parse through `readBody`/`bodyOf`
  (`Body`, a non-object reads as empty — a JSON `null` body used to throw a 500 where it now answers
  the refusal), nested fields through `bodyOf`/`listOf`, every D1 read names its row, the settings
  are `Settings` (all strings), merecat's dials `MerecatCfg` (the ChatRoom's local copy retired),
  live events `HubEvent`, the DM answers the client's own `app/wire.ts` shapes, the analytics answer
  reads through accessors (a missing field is 0, no longer a thrown report), and
  `pure.js`/`webpush.js` became TypeScript (a `.js` module is `any` to every caller; the Workers
  types spell ECDH's `public` as `$public`, so the parameters travel as a named object rather than a
  cast). Typing found one more latent failure: `notifyDiscordForum` named an anonymous post with
  `displayName(null)`, which throws — unreachable while `ALLOW_ANON` is false, fixed as its sibling
  already was. Then the brand: `Env` carries a type-only `unique symbol` that rows (`AuthoredRow`),
  live events (`HubEvent`) and answers (`json<T>(body: T & EnvFree<T>)`) refuse, so the bug's own
  shape — `withNames(env, …)`, `json({ ...env })` — no longer compiles;
  `comments-worker/types/env_brand.check.ts` holds `@ts-expect-error` on each, which fails the build
  the day the refusal stops working (tried: without the brand, five of them go unused).
  contact-worker's Env lost its open index signature. `tests/js/any_ratchet.test.mjs` now counts
  exactly — the `any` words minus those left after `module.stripTypeScriptTypes` — and holds the
  workers at zero with no `.js`, the client at its measured 2,482.

- **The challenge was asking nobody anything: established now means a challenge was passed
  (2026-09-17)** — found while reporting the leak review's findings, fixed at the owner's word.
  `isEstablished` read "has a `profiles` row, a comment or a wall post", and since 2026-09-16 a
  profiles row is what ANY keyed read leaves behind (`registerMember`, the one road a member row
  appears by). So a key one minute old was established by opening the board once — and because the
  client asks `/config`, which carries only the global sparing switch, it mounted a widget for
  NOBODY. Turnstile stood on every write path and challenged no one: 54 profiles rows stood where 8
  identities had ever written anything. It leaked nothing and let no one read anything — it is the
  bot gate, and it was open. Migration 0018 adds `profiles.verified_at`, and `verifyTurnstile`
  stamps it the moment siteverify says yes; `isEstablished` is that column and nothing else, which
  also restores what the upload, call and first-DM gates always claimed to mean ("after your first
  post or profile save"). The backfill keeps every existing member exempt — a post, a feed post, a
  DM send and the profile save are all Turnstile-gated, so anyone who has ever written here is
  spared, and a bare row is not; the kit's three `HIDDEN_HASHES` identities are stamped by hash
  because no scripted browser can earn one (webtest/POLICY.md: rotating a test key means stamping
  it). The client now asks a question about ITSELF: `/prefs` answers `turnstile.spared` — the whole
  rule as the server will apply it to the next write — and the remembered answer belongs to the
  identity it was given for, so a key change re-asks rather than inheriting a spare it never earned.
  A hint only: the server decides again on every write, as `Domain.Turnstile` always said. The tests
  hold the shape of the hole — a row from a read spares nothing, a passed challenge writes the
  record once and a later one never moves it — and `establish()` in the harness now stamps, with
  `seen()` beside it for what a read leaves.

## Build system

- **The corpus sources ride a GitHub Release, not git (2026-09-16)** — the review's P2-6: 364 MB of
  `resources/` was tracked — 237 generated `*-body.tex` (272 MB; the converters' output, committed
  once so nobody had to run them), 50 preserved `*-src.html`, 30 ThML/XML sources, 8 source PDFs —
  and every clone was a 190 MB download for texts nobody edits by hand. They are now four `.tar.xz`
  shards on the GitHub Release `sources` of this public repository (body-tex 86 MB, src-html 17 MB,
  pdf 12.6 MB, xml 4.6 MB — 120 MB in all, xz preset 3), each pinned by sha256 in
  `resources/SOURCES.json` (tracked; every file's sha256, bytes and shard). `scripts/sources.py` is
  the whole tool: `manifest`, `pack`, `publish` (the dev box's `gh`, done BEFORE the manifest is
  committed, so the asset exists before the build that needs it), `fetch` (takes a shard from
  `local/sources/` when it is there and matches, else downloads it anonymously from the release —
  the repository is public, so a `pull_request` build fetches with no secret and Bot Fight Mode is
  never in the way; verifies the tarball, extracts into `resources/`, verifies every file), `check`.
  `make fetch-sources` runs first under `make html`; `build.yml` caches `local/sources/` by the
  manifest's hash and fetches before the PDF and site steps. Why a release and not R2: the build
  runs on pull requests, which carry no secret (Principle 3), and a runner is 403'd by the zone's
  Bot Fight Mode — a release asset needs neither. The bodies stay STORED, not regenerated in CI (the
  converters remain the hand road; `make -C resources body`, then manifest · pack · publish · commit
  the manifest). Proven: a file moved aside came back byte-identical from the local tarball; a file
  moved aside with its shard removed came back byte-identical from the release download;
  `tests/py/test_sources_manifest.py` holds every body and source under `resources/` listed and
  untracked, every shard a published asset, a present file matching its sha. The history rewrite
  that would shrink existing clones (189 MB → an estimated 40) is a separate act the owner
  authorises, as on 2026-09-09.

- **The compiler comes by pinned hash, and the npm toolchain is watched (2026-09-17)** — the
  standing review's `npm audit` read ten advisories where the 09-16 review had read zero: one
  critical (`tar`), eight high, one moderate, every one in the build toolchain, and nobody watching
  — `.github/dependabot.yml` covered only the actions. Six left with bumps — `wrangler` 4.118 →
  4.133 (its miniflare, undici and sharp), `esbuild` 0.24.2 → 0.28.2 (the dev-server advisory;
  wrangler had been building the worker with 0.28.1 all along), `lit` 3.2.1 → 3.3.3 — and the
  bundles stayed byte-deterministic (two `make bundle` runs identical) and inside their budgets
  (app.js 285,820 B, comments.js 242,270 B, the ceilings untouched). The other four (`tar` ≤ 7.5.20,
  `cacache`, `make-fetch-happen`, `purescript-installer`) hung off the npm `purescript` package,
  whose installer subclasses tar 6's `Unpack` (`dl-tar/index.js`); npm's only "fix" was a downgrade
  of the compiler to 0.14, and a forced tar 7 under a subclass of its internals is a gamble. So the
  compiler comes the way the corpus sources do: `scripts/toolchain.py` reads
  `tests/_support/toolchain.json` (the version, the release URL, and for linux-x64, darwin-x64 and
  darwin-arm64 the tarball's sha256 AND the binary's), downloads on a miss, verifies the tarball,
  reads the ONE member `purescript/purs` out of it — never an extraction by member name, so nothing
  a hostile archive says about paths is obeyed — verifies the binary and writes `local/bin/purs`; a
  matching copy costs no network. The linux pin was cross-checked byte-for-byte against the npm
  package's `purs.bin` before that package left `package.json` together with its `allowScripts`
  approval. `make toolchain` is the road, `psbuild` depends on it, the npm `build:ps` script puts
  `local/bin` on PATH for spago, both workflows cache `local/bin` under the pin file's hash and
  fetch before they compile, and the PureScript output cache key includes the pin file so a compiler
  bump invalidates it. `tests/py/test_toolchain.py` holds the pin's shape, the verify road (a
  synthetic tarball: accepted with its pins, refused with either wrong or the member missing) and
  the workflow order. The watch: the npm ecosystem joins Dependabot (development dependencies
  grouped weekly for minor and patch; a major, or `lit`, alone) beside the security updates
  Terraform already declares; `npm audit` reads zero and the repository's alerts page is empty; the
  policy is CICD.md §2.5. Two stale comments went with it — the Makefile's "Lit vendored under
  vendor/" and spago.yaml's "vendored purs (vendor/purs/)": both had come from npm for months.

- **One owner for every share card, and a card that says which page it is (2026-09-17)** — the
  platform review's metadata finding in its smallest, cheapest part. Measured on the built tree:
  234 of the site's 274 pages carried the identical `og:description`, "A primary source in the Mere
  Catholicity Library", so a shared link to Virgil and a shared link to Irenaeus read the same below
  their titles; `og:title` was already per-page and right on all 274. The cards also came from three
  owners, which is why `partials/social-bishop.html` looked like one article's private social
  markup:
  pandoc's `-H` is the only door into a `<head>` pandoc writes itself, `book.html` had exactly the
  same arrangement in `partials/social.html`, and `scripts/inject_social.py` — which sweeps every
  `docs/*.html` after the build — skipped both because they already carried an `og:title`. The
  description is now derived: the work's own title and the shelf it stands on, read from
  `docs/library.html` through `library_order.py`'s `Catalog` (the page that IS the catalog a reader
  browses, so the card and the shelf cannot disagree) — "The Aeneid of Virgil — from The
  philosophers, and the religion of Rome, in the Mere Catholicity Library." 233 of the 234 were
  named
  there; the tree now holds 269 distinct descriptions over 271 carded pages, and the two remaining
  pairs are curated on purpose (home shares the book's line, the feed shares the board's). The owner
  chose the mechanical formula over 27 hand-written shelf sentences, so the Schaff volumes repeat a
  fuller `og:title` and every corpus work is still `og:type` `book` with `cover.jpg` as its image —
  a 1200x630 banner and a per-shelf voice are both open, and neither needs a page touched. Three
  things had broken silently. The card now sits between `<!--mc-card-->` fences and is REWRITTEN in
  place: without them a pages tree restored from the build cache keeps whatever formula last wrote
  it
  for ever, because every page has an `og:title` and so every page looks curated (a shim strips the
  old unfenced block by exact bytes for one month — `tests/_support/retirements.json`, due
  2026-10-17). `away.html`, the off-site door, had a *committed* injection announcing itself as a
  primary source in the Library; a `noindex` page is now never given a card at all, and the two
  retired partials live in `OVERRIDES` beside every other page's card, with `partials/head.html`
  holding what is not a card (the favicon `bishop-presbyter.html` never had). And `annals.html` and
  `histories.html` had no card whatever — built after the last sweep ran. What guards it is a sweep,
  not a sample, because the class of bug is "one page was fixed and the other 233 were not":
  `tests/py/test_social_cards.py` holds every indexable page to a card naming its own address, every
  Library page to naming its shelf, no description to more than two pages, a `noindex` page to none,
  and the shim's bytes to what actually shipped.

## Hosting and delivery

- **The platform reviewed whole, and the finding is the gap between the machine and its use
  (2026-09-17).** The owner asked for a full codebase and feature review with priorities; it is
  `docs/architecture/reviews/2026-09-17-platform-review.md`, measured against `b58fa65` and against
  production the same afternoon. The tree is healthy — `make tests` green, `npm audit` zero, no
  `TODO` anywhere in the hand-written sources, the workers holding no `any` — and production
  answered 4 categories, 6 topics, 78 posts, last post nine days old, and 15 identities. Three
  things came out of the measuring that the documents did not already say. **One security chain,
  live and unauthenticated:** the account secret's public form is `SHA-256(key)` unsalted
  (`lib.ts:294`), that hash is the member's public identifier on every board read, and
  `GET /api/comments/dm/directory` serves the whole roster of them with no key at all — while
  sign-in accepts any key of 16 characters and the weak-key check is a client-side toast that never
  refuses (`client/profile.ts:594`, `app/appchrome.ts:1606`). Harvest the roster, run a wordlist
  offline, and any member who typed a memorable key is impersonable; `ADMIN_HASHES` ships in the
  bundle, so the admin is the most exposed target. The remedy is three layers — key the directory
  (an hour), refuse weak keys on both sides and audit the fifteen live hashes (half a day), then
  stop publishing the hash of the secret at all: a stored `profiles.pubid` =
  `SHA-256(pepper || hash)` served in its place, the account hash staying D1's primary key
  (two to three days). **Ninety-one served pages are over a megabyte:** `anf03.html` transfers
  1.56 MB compressed and parses into 56,425 elements, and every one of them also pulls ~101 KB
  gzipped of application shell a reader of Ante-Nicene Fathers will never use. Those are the pages
  search sends people to. **And the metadata is wrong at scale:** `lang=""` on 237 of 274 pages (a
  WCAG 3.1.1 Level A failure on 86% of the site), more than one `<h1>` on 230, `rel="canonical"` on
  one, no skip link on documents with a 34-entry table of contents, no RSS at any of the five
  conventional addresses, no email capture, and no analytics at all — though
  `static.cloudflareinsights.com` has been allowlisted in the zone CSP all along. The
  recommendation is a sequence, not a list: close the chain, serve the readers who are already
  arriving (split the volumes, fix the metadata, turn the meter on), give them somewhere to go
  (server-render `/t/<id>-<slug>` through the `handleHandleCard` HTMLRewriter pattern the `/@handle`
  route already proves, publish a feed, capture an address), and only then resume the write-path
  port — with P1 pulled forward and merecat and the DM thread, a third of its 23 days, pushed back
  behind everything that has readers on the other end of it.

- **The app reloaded itself because the edge rewrites every page it serves (2026-09-17).** The
  owner, in the installed app: "a while after navigating around the community or my profile page I
  tapped home to go back to home and it subsequently refreshed itself even showing the splash
  screen" — not every time, and "the splash screen should never show itself after initial app open".
  The splash has exactly one road: the inline `mc-fout` head script, which runs only on a full
  document load of Home. So something was reloading the document. It was the service worker, by way
  of Cloudflare. `docs/sw.js` holds the seven tab skeletons, serves a cached one instantly and
  revalidates it against the origin in the background; when the fresh bytes differ from the cached
  ones it updates the cache and tells every open page (`mc-page-updated`), and `docs/nav.js` heals a
  page under 30 s old with one reload — the cure for an installed app running days-old markup. The
  bytes ALWAYS differ. This zone runs Cloudflare's JavaScript Detections (`enable_js` in
  `terraform/zone.tf`, kept deliberately through the Turnstile postmortem), which appends a
  `<script>` carrying a per-RESPONSE ray id — `window.__CF$cv$params={r:'a3ce532a0f8964b6',…}` —
  to every HTML body: three fetches of `/index.html` measured that afternoon returned three distinct
  bodies, one line apart, and a fetch-style request (`Sec-Fetch-Dest: empty`, the shape the worker's
  own revalidation sends) varies exactly the same way. So every hop onto a tab page "discovered" a
  change, and any document still inside its 30-second heal window reloaded — on Home, replaying the
  launch screen. The first hop after a launch is precisely the one that fetches, the shell's
  in-memory document cache being empty then, which is why it read as a while after navigating
  around; the heal's own guards (30 s, twice per five minutes, once per cause per 20 s, never
  mid-typing) are why it was not every time. The 2026-09-16 crumb-ring note that `heal-reload page:`
  might be "Cloudflare's varying JSD line — unverified" was right, and is now measured. **The fix is
  to compare the document THE SITE serves**: `siteBytes` in `docs/sw.js` takes any `<script>` naming
  `/cdn-cgi/` or `__CF$cv$params` out of both sides before the comparison, so a deploy still differs
  and a ray id no longer does — and a page that has not changed no longer costs a cache write and
  four `postMessage` rounds on every hop. `tests/js/sw_revalidate.test.mjs` runs that very function
  (sliced out of the worker by two asserted anchors) against the injected block captured verbatim
  from a live response, and guards the other half too: strip one `<script>` too many and a change to
  the inline theme/splash script would stop being seen. **And a reload is not a launch**: the head
  script (`scripts/inject_social.py`) now paints the splash only when the navigation type is not
  `reload`, so any future self-reload — a heal after a real deploy, a triple pull, a cache clear —
  comes back in place instead of looking like the app restarting itself. Only the reload case is
  spared, the one no engine disagrees about; a resume or a relaunch still gets its launch screen,
  and the FOUT gate is never spared, a reloaded Home painting its static markup exactly as a launch
  does. The inline script's CSP hash moved with it (`scripts/csp_hashes.py` →
  `terraform/rulesets.tf`, report-only, so the window before the apply costs reports rather than a
  blocked script).
## The PureScript application layer

- **The read transport leaves the boot, and the shell stops waiting for the client it serves
  (2026-09-18).** The write-path port's P1, first slice, and the reason it was pulled ahead of the
  rest: `app/api.ts` — the documented SDK every new feature is supposed to call — had no transport
  of its own. It was HANDED one at wire-up, and the hand belonged to `window.mcKit`, which the
  classic boot does not assemble until its last line, so `app/shell.ts` polled every 500 ms for up
  to eight seconds waiting for the client to appear in order to give itself its own `fetch`. Any
  read the shell made inside that window went unkeyed. `app/transport.ts` now holds the five the
  boot owned — `fetchRetry` (the bounded retry whose per-attempt timeout is the cure for the
  "Loading…" that hung for ever on a flaky radio), `freshOpts`/`stampFresh`, `freshParam`, and the
  store-backed `cachedJson`/`peekJson` — moved verbatim, over a STATIC import of `app/store.ts`.
  That static import is the whole point: `cachedJson` and `peekJson` each opened with an
  `if (window.mcStore)` fallback that has rendered for nobody since the bundle was ruled required,
  and with the store imported there is no longer an ordering hazard to lose a race to. The 27 call
  sites in `app/views/*` import the functions; the poll is deleted and replaced by a lazy getter;
  the kit fell 71 → 65 members (`cachedJson`, `fetchRetry`, `freshOpts`, `freshParam`, `peekJson`,
  `stampFresh`), which is the direction the port says it may only go. **Two rules the move creates,
  both silent, both swept by `tests/js/transport.test.mjs`.** ONE STORE: `client/` and `app/` are
  separate esbuild graphs, so a *value* import from `client/` into `app/` bundles a second copy of
  the cache into `docs/comments.js` — two caches that never invalidate each other, no symptom until
  a stale read weeks later. The classic client therefore reaches the module at `window.mcTransport`
  through six one-line doors, and the sweep allows only `import type` across that line (the one
  that exists, `client/dm-inbox.ts` → `app/wire.ts`, is safe precisely because `wire.ts` declares
  no runtime value — a second test holds it to that). NEVER CAPTURE `fetch`: the shell wraps
  `window.fetch` (`app/wirecheck.ts`), so a module-level `const f = fetch` would freeze the
  unwrapped one and opt every read out of the wire check silently. The classic delegation is wired
  at FIRST USE, not at boot top: under `?app=0` the client boots without waiting for `app.js`, and a
  hard dereference there would have been a new way to fail where today only an actual read can.
  `tests/js/api.test.mjs` stops injecting a fake transport and stubs the global `fetch` instead —
  a stronger test, since the real ladder now runs and the stub only works BECAUSE of the
  never-capture rule. The client `any` baseline fell 2482 → 2475.

- **The edge injects a DIFFERENT token into a browser's copy, and the first fix met only half of it
  (2026-09-18).** The cure shipped the night before — strip the `<script>` Cloudflare's JavaScript
  Detections appends, then compare — was verified against production before it was believed, and it
  failed: seven cached pages, seven pairs of live responses, every pair still unequal after the
  filter. What a real browser is served is not what curl is served. Headless Chromium against prod,
  the worker unregistered through the site's own `?app=0` latch so the page's fetches reach the
  network: `/index.html`, `/community.html`, `/profile.html` and `/messages.html` each returned two
  different bodies, and the difference was a hidden crawler-protection link — `<a
  href="…/cdn-cgi/content?id=<token>" aria-hidden="true" rel="nofollow noopener"
  style="display:none">` right after `<body>`, a fresh token every response — with no JSD script
  anywhere in the document. curl, meanwhile, gets the JSD script and no honeypot. Two injections,
  two audiences, one behaviour: the body is never twice the same. (A first browser probe read
  "identical bytes" and nearly closed the question the wrong way — those fetches had been answered
  by our own service worker, which serves `/index.html?anything` from one cache key. A measurement
  of the network has to leave the worker out of it.) So the cure is now in two parts, because the
  third injection will arrive and this must not come back with it. `siteBytes` drops any `<script>`
  naming `/cdn-cgi/` and collapses every remaining `/cdn-cgi/` URL to its bare path, which takes the
  honeypot's token with it. And `codeKeys` decides who is TOLD: `mc-page-updated` — the message that
  reloads a reader's page — is sent only when the `?v=` keys the document carries have moved, which
  is the only honest definition of "you are running old code" (nav.js's own key moves with every
  asset, `MC_ASSETS` being part of its bytes). Markup that moves without them refreshes the cache
  and wakes nobody. Whatever the edge invents next can cost a needless cache write; it can no longer
  cost a reader their page. `tests/js/sw_revalidate.test.mjs` carries the honeypot verbatim and an
  invented third injection.
## The PureScript application layer

- **The frozen twins are deleted, and the kernel is the only copy that runs (2026-09-18).** P1's
  second slice, and the one CLAUDE.md already had a law for — *shared constants, tables and
  validators live in `Domain/*`; never re-inline a copy*. The classic client had been breaking it
  in the one way nobody notices: each helper read the kernel **behind a guard**, with a frozen
  hand-written copy behind the `else`. `if (window.mcRich) return window.mcRich.appendRich(…)` then
  sixty lines of a second body renderer; `if (window.mcCore) return window.mcCore.authIsMember(…)`
  then the hand conjunction; `(window.mcCore && window.mcCore.profileLimits) || { nick: 40, bio:
  500, sig: 200 }` — a literal that the comment above it admits had already drifted once. None of
  those `else` branches has executed since the bundle was ruled required in Wave F: the boot waits
  for `mc-shell-ready` and reads the kernel unconditionally, so a twin could only ever drift, never
  run. Retired in this slice: `appendRich` and `fillBody` (the body renderer, CODEBASE.md's largest
  measured clone at 78.8% of `richtext.ts`), the 80-line `scriptureHover` fallback IIFE, the
  `BIBLE` book-name map, `INLINE_BASE`/`INLINE_MD`, `scriptureDecor`, `ensureEmojiStyles`,
  `collectMentions`, `isMuted`, `toggleMute`, `isMember`, `blockedMessage`, `profileLimits`,
  `handleMax`, `canEdit`, `canDelete`, `mediaAcceptFor`, `authIsAdmin`, `authGate` — and
  **`classicRoute`**, a hand-written JS twin of `Domain.Route.parseRoute`, the site's whole URL→view
  ladder kept in duplicate. About 330 lines gone; `docs/comments.js` fell 237.0 KB → 226.9 KB, its
  budget ceiling clicks 247,107 → 231,484 B, and the client `any` baseline 2475 → 2451. The DM
  family and merecat keep their guards for now: they are P6 and P5, parked behind the phases that
  have readers, and their guards test for individual kernel MEMBERS rather than for the kernel —
  a different shape, and one that deserves its own slice rather than a drive-by.

## Cloudflare Workers (dynamic backend)

- **Somewhere to go: a thread is a page now (2026-09-17)** — the review's P2, the half of it that
  is mechanical. Ninety-odd conversations lived at `community.html?topic=<n>`, a URL whose body
  arrives by fetch after the client boots: to a crawler, one empty page wearing the same title
  ninety times, and to a feed reader, nothing at all — all five conventional feed addresses 404'd.
  `/@handle` already proved the road (fetch the static page from the origin, inject through
  `HTMLRewriter`), so `comments-worker/src/routes/seo.ts` takes it three times: `/t/<id>-<slug>`
  is `community.html` with the thread's TEXT in it — title, share card, `rel=canonical`, the topic
  and its first fifty replies as escaped paragraphs where the board section stands; `/feed`,
  `/feed.xml`, `/rss`, `/rss.xml` and `/atom.xml` are one RSS feed of the fifty newest live board
  posts, each linked to its thread's own URL; `/sitemap-threads.xml` names every live thread, and
  `docs/robots.txt` names it beside the static sitemap. The id decides and the slug is cosmetic, so
  a retitled topic never breaks a link. Three details are load-bearing. The page is one directory
  deep and every asset and link the client writes is relative to the site root, so the worker
  prepends `<base href="/">` and the client removes it on boot, replaces the URL with
  `community.html?topic=<id>` and renders the topic view as always — the reader lands on the app,
  not on a second rendering of it. Nothing here reads an identity: what it serves is exactly what
  the public RSS feed already served (a `live` topic in a board category, never the back room,
  never a held or shadowed post), so the three roads share the feed's rules rather than inventing
  a second set. One trap, found by probing prod: a worker route pattern with no
  `*` matches the URL EXACTLY, query string and all, so `/feed.xml?utm=x` fell
  straight through to Pages and 404'd — every feed address now carries a trailing
  `*`. Bare `/feed` cannot: the API refuses a `?` in a pattern (10022) and
  `/feed*` would swallow `/feed.html`, the social feed page, so that one address
  answers only without a query, and `/feed.xml` is the one to publish. And the crawl is the
  worker's request to pay for, so `robots.txt` keeps the rule it
  already kept for the forum: Google may read the threads, every other bot may not. The leak
  sweep takes all three roads as four identities (the held post and the back room's topic must
  read as nonexistence), `tests/worker/seo.test.mjs` holds the escaping and the four refusals, and
  the test harness's MiniRewriter grew `prepend` and an html-safe `setInnerContent` to stand in for
  the real thing. Email capture, the third thing the review asked for, is NOT here: it wants the
  owner's decision (where an address is kept, what is ever sent to it, and the unsubscribe that
  must come with it) before a line of it is written.
## Hosting and delivery

- **An article page asks before it pays: the comments client is fetched only where a section is
  actually open (2026-09-18).** The write-path port's invariant 1 says a reader of a KJV chapter
  never downloads a write path; it turned out seven of the site's OWN writings were downloading one.
  A comments section is admin-switched and ships CLOSED (`Domain.Comments`, whose polarity is the
  opposite of the social switch: only a listed path opens anything), but every content page carried
  an eager `<script type="module" src="comments.js">`. So a reader of `credo.html`, `about.html`,
  `lex-orandi.html`, `book.html`, `objections.html` or either charting page downloaded **227 KB** of
  forum, DM, composer, admin-console and librarian client — whereupon `comments.js` booted, read
  `/config`, found its path unlisted and rendered nothing. Production the same afternoon answered
  `comments.pages = []` and `social.enabled = false`: not one page was open, and the whole cost was
  being paid for a certainty. The decision moves ahead of the download. `scripts/content.py` and
  `partials/book-tail.html` now emit the MOUNT alone; `app/shell.ts` gains `commentsClientIfOpen`,
  which on the first paint and on every soft navigation looks for a `section[data-comments]`, reads
  the config — **the same read `comments.js` used to make afterwards**, same URL, same five-minute
  TTL, deduped through `app/store.ts`, so this costs no extra request — and injects the client by
  `window.mcAsset('comments.js')` only when the path is listed. `comments.js` therefore joins
  `RUNTIME_ASSETS` in the stamper so its key rides `MC_ASSETS`; it stays in `PAGE_ASSETS` too,
  because the eight hand-written app pages are the client's own and keep their eager tag. Both
  navigation roads agree for free: `pageScripts()` reads the incoming document's tags, so a page
  with no tag is a page the soft road does not fetch either. A failed injection clears both flags so
  the next visit tries again, and an unreachable config reads as CLOSED — the same direction the
  worker's own refusals take. `COMMENTS_V` retires with its last reader.
  `tests/py/test_comments_client_lazy.py` sweeps the served tree (only the seven app pages may carry
  it eagerly, and they must), holds both generators to the mount, and holds the stamper and the
  shell to the runtime key — because the regression is invisible: a generator quietly re-adding the
  tag costs 227 KB and breaks nothing. **A note for the next agent:** the stamp-restore dance before
  a commit (`git checkout -- docs partials`) will silently revert a DELIBERATE edit under those
  paths. Restore by naming the stamped files, never the directory, and run the gate AFTER the
  restore — this passage and `book-tail.html` were both lost to it once, and only the gate's second
  run found them.

- **The paintings are in the cache before the finger arrives, and the lazy load that put them there
  is untouched (2026-09-18).** Nineteen pages carry a faded background painting — the six features,
  four content-page accents, and two pages that draw a pair (`charting-communions` = St Peter's
  Square + Hagia Sophia, `free-churches` = the Reformation Wall + St Giles') — thirteen names,
  twenty-six files, 1.2 MB of -m and 3.1 MB of -d, and they are the ONLY background images on the
  site: `styles/main.css` holds every `url()` there is. They load lazily by construction, because a
  painting is fetched only when `body[data-art="…"]` matches it, and that is the feature: a reader
  who opens nothing but the Library never pays for the Pietà. What lazy costs is the first arrival —
  the download begins at the moment the page is shown, so Profile and Inbox fade their art in a beat
  late, and on a phone every one of the six tabs is ONE TAP away from wherever the reader stands. So
  `app/artwarm.ts` walks the rest into the browser's cache: the six tab paintings first (a tap is
  quicker than a link), then the seven a link reaches, one at a time at `fetchPriority: 'low'`, and
  only the -m or -d half this viewport will actually use. The politeness is the design. It begins
  after the `load` event — the current painting is a CSS background of a matched element, so it is
  part of that event — and then only when `requestIdleCallback` says the main thread is free; it
  walks sequentially, so the reader's own next navigation never queues behind thirteen paintings; a
  refusal costs that one file and not the walk; the art switched off (Settings → Appearance),
  `Save-Data`, or a `2g` link downloads nothing at all, and the setting is read when the walk FIRES,
  not when it is scheduled, because seconds pass in between. One walk per document, latched in the
  module rather than the caller: `installChrome()` runs once per document today, but `mcBoot`
  re-runs on every soft navigation and a warm per hop would be a slow drip of needless requests. The
  URLs are root-relative (`/theme/…`), which is what `url(theme/…)` in `/style.css` resolves to — a
  different spelling of the same file is a second cache entry and the whole point would be lost. Two
  lists now name the same paintings, so `tests/js/art_warm.test.mjs` sweeps `styles/main.css` in
  both directions and against the files on disk: a painting added to the stylesheet and forgotten
  here would always arrive late, and a name left here after the stylesheet dropped it would be a 404
  fetched for ever. The breakpoint is read from the stylesheet too — warming -d below 900px would be
  twice the bytes and none of them used.
## Hosting and delivery

- **The meter was installed in July and measured nothing, because the thing that installs it had
  been deleted (2026-09-18).** The platform review's last P1 item was "no analytics at all", and the
  reason it read that way from outside is that it was true from outside: a fetch of the live home
  page and of `/about.html` with a browser UA found zero occurrences of `cloudflareinsights`. The
  account API told a different story — `GET /accounts/<id>/rum/site_info/list` returned a Web
  Analytics site for this zone, created 2026-07-17, `enabled: true`, `auto_install: true`, its own
  token and snippet — so the meter had been turned on two months ago and the dashboard had been
  empty ever since. The join between the two stories is the site record's `ruleset` field: it names
  zone ruleset `4f4f6eeb-b8bf-4318-a5d2-aa5840b7b4a2`, and `GET /zones/<zone>/rulesets/4f4f…`
  answers `not_found`. Automatic setup works by a ruleset of Cloudflare's own that injects the
  beacon into HTML at the edge; that ruleset is gone, the record still says it is there, and nothing
  anywhere goes red — the graph simply shows no visitors, which is exactly what a site with no
  visitors looks like. So the beacon ships from the build instead. `docs/nav.js` — the one script
  every page already loads, and the same reason `deeplink.js` lives there — appends the module
  script with the site token in the URL (the form Cloudflare documents for a beacon injected by
  another script: a module has no `document.currentScript` to read a `data-cf-beacon` attribute
  from). The token is public by design, like the Turnstile sitekeys. Two gates: nothing is sent from
  a hostname that is not `merecatholicity.com`, and nothing is sent when `navigator.webdriver` is
  true — the nightly headless run against production would otherwise post itself into the numbers
  every night and bend every "which page first" decision toward our own robot. The CSP needed no
  change at all: `static.cloudflareinsights.com` in `script-src` and `cloudflareinsights.com` in
  `connect-src` (manual installation reports to the latter, not to `/cdn-cgi/rum`) have been
  allowlisted since the ruleset was written, for a beacon that was never served.
  `terraform/analytics.tf` adopts the site by import and sets `auto_install = false`, which is the
  half of this that is a rule rather than a fix: two roads to one meter would count every view
  twice, invisibly, and Cloudflare's own note is that only one snippet may be rendered per page.
  `tests/py/test_analytics.py` holds all of it — the tag, the token, the two gates, the CSP, and the
  `false`.

## Build system

- **The metadata every page owes a reader who is not us (2026-09-18).** Three of the review's P1
  defects were one line each and all of them were sitewide, which is why none had ever been fixed:
  `lang=""` on 237 of 274 pages, more than one `<h1>` on 230, `rel="canonical"` on exactly one.
  `scripts/page_meta.py` is the one sweep that answers all three plus the skip link, running at the
  end of `make html` after `strip-nav` and `sync-index` (both rewrite pages) and before the sitemap.
  It works over the BUILT tree rather than in the forty-odd pandoc stanzas and the two generated
  `.mk` files that produce it — the same reasoning `inject_social.py` was written under, and the
  reason a new corpus page gets all four for nothing. **lang:** pandoc writes `lang=""` when the
  document declares none, and an empty language is worse than a missing one — it is a WCAG 3.1.1
  (Level A) failure a screen reader obeys by choosing no voice. A page that names a language keeps
  it. **canonical:** the value is the page's own `og:url`, which the build has written for years, so
  the two cannot disagree; it goes LAST in the head, not beside the line it was read from, because
  on 235 pages that line lives inside `inject_social.py`'s card fence and a fence is rewritten
  wholesale every build — a canonical placed inside one would be deleted by the next run of a script
  that knows nothing about it. `resources.html`, the one page that already had a canonical (pointing
  at `library.html`, deliberately), is never overwritten. **one h1:** every LaTeX `\section*` became
  an `<h1>` beside the title block's own, so each heading in a pandoc page is demoted one level,
  leaving the title as the document's single `<h1>` and the divisions ranked beneath it. Pandoc's
  ids are untouched, so every deep link, TOC entry and merecat citation still lands — and `main` is
  marked `class="prose corpus"`, which is both what the stylesheet keys the UNCHANGED visual scale
  to (a corpus `h2` still looks exactly like the old `h1`) and the done-mark that stops a second
  pass shifting the corpus down again, every build, until the Fathers were ranked `h6`. Two traps
  are worth naming because both were found by measuring rather than by reading: pandoc wraps long
  tags across lines, so 524 of `anf03.html`'s 774 chapter headings are `<h4\nid="…">` and a regex
  that stops at the newline demotes a third of a document and reports success; and the demotion is a
  rewrite of output, so idempotence is not a nicety but the whole safety of running it over a
  cache-restored tree. **skip link:** first inside `<main>` on any corpus page with a table of
  contents, its target right after the TOC — both inside `main` because the app shell replaces that
  whole element on a soft navigation, and a link left outside it would survive a hop into a page
  whose target it no longer has. 271 pages swept: 237 given a language, 270 a canonical, 235
  re-ranked, 233 made skippable.

## Cloudflare Workers (dynamic backend)

- **The roster stops being an anonymous read: the first of the P0's three layers (2026-09-18)** —
  the 2026-09-17 review's only urgent finding is one chain, and the chain's first link was a door.
  A member's account secret is a key held in `localStorage`; the server stores and publishes
  `SHA-256(key)`, unsalted and one round; that same digest is the member's PUBLIC id, on every
  comment, every board topic and every feed post as `author_hash`. And `GET
  /api/comments/dm/directory` handed the entire roster of them to anyone who asked — no key, no
  Turnstile, no origin check (it was a GET), one read-limit token, and a `public, max-age=300`
  header inviting the edge to serve the next stranger from cache. Fifteen hashes, with join times
  and nicks, from the command line. The rest of the attack needs no request at all: unsalted
  single-round SHA-256 runs at billions of guesses a second on one consumer GPU, so every member
  who typed a memorable key instead of keeping the generated one is a wordlist away from being
  impersonated — and `ADMIN_HASHES` is a literal in the bundle, which makes the admin the
  best-marked target on the board. The route is `POST` now, behind `gated(… key: 'required')` with
  no block gate (parity with `/dm/threads`; a locked identity reading a roster harms nobody), and
  it carries no cache header at all — an answer a shared cache may keep is an answer a keyless
  reader can have. Six client call sites send the key, and the Members screen asks a visitor to
  join rather than showing a list that cannot load. **This does not close the hole and is not
  meant to**: a hash still rides every board and feed read, so harvesting is slower, not
  impossible, and a key here proves no membership — identities are self-minted, with no
  registration step, so a harvester can mint one and ask. Be exact about what layer one buys, then:
  the answer is no longer EDGE-CACHED, which is the real gain (a `max-age=300` roster was served to
  unlimited strangers without ever reaching the rate limiter; every ask costs a token now); it is no
  longer a URL a crawler or a bystander can open; and no other site can read it from a visiting
  member's browser. It is NOT proof against a script: the origin guard lets a missing Origin through
  by its own deliberate rule, so `curl` with any minted key still gets the list, bounded by the read
  limiter alone. Layer one is a door, not a wall, and the distinction is worth keeping straight.
  What it shuts is the one door that
  served EVERY member at once for free — including the lurkers whose only trace is a published DM
  key, who appear in no post anywhere. The anon reach
  floor in `env_leak.test.mjs` falls 19 → 18 deliberately, with the reason in the file, because a
  falling count is normally a sweep going hollow and this one is a door closing. Layer two (a real
  key floor, refused server-side) and layer three (stop publishing the hash of the secret at all)
  are the rest of the chain.

- **A real floor under the key, refused server-side: the P0's second layer (2026-09-18)** — layer
  one keyed the roster; it did not make a guessable key safe, because the hash on every board and
  feed read is still the key's unsalted digest. Layer two is the floor that was missing everywhere:
  the sign-in accepted any sixteen characters, and the only strength check was a client toast that
  never refused. The strength ladder already lived in the kernel (`Domain.Auth.keyStrength`:
  `Generated | Strong | Weak`); what it lacked was a decision. Three functions carry it now, and the
  difference between them IS the design. `keyAcceptable` is the one predicate (anything but `Weak` —
  twenty characters over three classes, or the generated forty-three), read by BOTH sides so the
  floor is stated once. `keyRefusal` is the server's answer on a WRITE, and only a write: a weak-key
  member must be able to sign in, look around and be told what to do, not meet a wall on a read.
  `keyWarning` is what the client ASKS at sign-in — a question, never a refusal, and this is the
  load-bearing subtlety: the key IS the account, there is no rotation road until layer three, so
  refusing a pasted weak key would lock an existing member out of their own posts, messages and
  profile on a new device. `keyFloor` is called from the shared preambles (`gated` / `keyed` /
  `adminGated`) AND from the sixteen write roads that roll their own — posting a comment, a feed
  post, a profile edit, the three uploads and the rest never touch the shared preamble, so a floor
  wired only into it would have left the site's MAIN writes wide open (the first cut did exactly
  that, and a live probe found it: a weak key still posted a comment). The admin console is floored
  too — a cracked admin key that cannot write is exactly the point. Every POST_LIMIT road is swept
  in `tests/worker/key_floor_reach.test.mjs`: a weak identity is driven down a write from each route
  file and refused, and a source law fails if any handler rate-limits a write without a `keyFloor`
  beside it, so a new road cannot be added unprotected — a floor with a hole is not a floor. Two
  things it must NOT do, both caught by a
  test: refuse the anonymous empty key (that is `key: 'optional'`'s anonymity, not a weak identity,
  and every such handler would answer 400 about strength), and spend a D1 read on a good key (strong
  and generated keys never reach the `profiles` lookup). The refusal is staged: a weak key with
  history behind it still writes until **2026-10-18**, named in `tests/_support/retirements.json` so
  the suite goes red that morning and the choice to lock anyone out is made deliberately, not by
  a date sliding past unnoticed. And the diagnostic that gates layer three ships with it —
  `scripts/key_audit.py` runs a wordlist against the live roster exactly as an attacker would (it IS
  the attack, run by the defender): a member it cracks is one to contact and a reason to treat layer
  three as urgent; a clean run over a large list is the evidence the generated-key default is
  holding and layer three can be scheduled rather than rushed. The test fixtures' own keys were
  lengthened to clear the floor (`identity()` in the worker harness), because a fixture the worker
  refuses on every write would be testing the refusal, not the road.

## Hosting and delivery

- **A volume is a shelf now: ninety-one pages over a megabyte become an index and ten thousand
  readable ones (2026-09-18).** The platform review's P1 named the measurement — `anf03.html`
  transferred 1.56 MB compressed and parsed into 56,425 elements, and ninety-one served pages were
  over a megabyte — and named the pattern too: `summa.html` is a 5 KB index over five part pages,
  and the pattern was simply never applied anywhere else. `scripts/split_volumes.py` applies it to
  every oversized volume, over the BUILT tree rather than in the forty-odd pandoc stanzas and two
  generated `.mk` files that produce it, which is the same reasoning `inject_social.py` was written
  under and the reason a new corpus page gets the treatment for nothing. The numbers: 150 volumes,
  10,364 parts, the median part **13.8 KB** and the 90th percentile 76 KB, three parts still over a
  megabyte (down from ninety-one) because three divisions offer no heading to cut at. `anf03.html`
  is 10 KB. The corpus grew 22% in bytes (294 MB to 359 MB) — the per-part head, nav and footer —
  and the build grew about ninety seconds. **The depth is chosen per volume, not decreed**: the
  shallowest heading level whose largest part comes under 400 KB, else whichever level makes the
  largest part smallest, because the corpus is not uniform (`plutarch.html` is 68 lives and no
  subheadings at all; `npnf108.html` is one division and 153 subdivisions). A division still over
  600 KB — Against Marcion, the Stromata, Augustine's letters — is cut a SECOND time at its own next
  level and its parts wear it as a crumb, which is what takes the seventeen remaining megabyte pages
  down to three without serving the whole of anf03 as 774 chapter cards. **Nothing that was ever
  linked may break**, and that is most of the work: every contents entry on the index now WEARS the
  id it links to (pandoc's own `toc-` prefix dropped, so it costs nothing) — `anf03.html#apology.`
  still resolves for the link checker, for a reader with no JavaScript, and as the hop `deeplink.js`
  reads; the ids below the contents live in a `<volume>-anchors.json` the hop fetches only on a
  miss, because as stubs in the page they were 124 KB of a 134 KB index (a Father's chapter ids are
  whole sentences); a cross-reference from one part into another is rewritten and one inside a part
  is left exactly as it was; and the footnotes follow their references keeping their ORIGINAL
  numbers as `<li value="…">`, since the superscript in the text is literal and a subset list
  renumbered from 1 misnumbers every note on the page. Three traps were found only by measuring.
  Pandoc wraps long tags across lines, so `<li\nid="fn3517">` is ordinary and a space-only pattern
  took 3,523 of npnf114's notes and left the rest — fifteen marks pointing at nothing, and the link
  checker was the only thing that said so. The part mark must be read from the WHOLE file, not a 4
  KB sniff of its head: the anti-flash script and the share card put it kilobytes down, so the sniff
  found no part anywhere, stale parts were never swept and an oversized part was a volume to the
  next run. And a division whose own TITLE carries a footnote leaves it behind, because the title
  travels into the title block and the notes were collected from what followed it. **The librarian
  reads the parts and cites the volume.** merecat's citations are `<volume>.html#<anchor>` by
  construction, and the index hops them onward, so nothing about the shelf, the tiers or the
  citation format changes: `librarian/ingest.py` resolves a split volume to its index plus its parts
  and walks them as one document, with one used-id set across the volume and the ledger signature
  hashing every source — the parse version is bumped to 2, so the next `merecat.yml` re-ingests the
  corpus once. Both walkers learned the same new rule, and had to learn it together: a part's title
  block IS its division's heading, so the paragraphs before a part's first in-body heading are
  `<division>__p1` in the browser and in the replay alike, instead of `p__p1` in one and
  `<division>__p1` in the other. Measured against the unsplit volume: 3,462,505 bytes of chunk text
  before, 3,462,483 after — the twenty-two missing are the index's one line about itself, which both
  walkers skip.

## Hosting and delivery

- **A reading page waits for the shell; it does not wait on it (2026-09-18).** The other half of the
  review's P1: `nav.js` loads `chrome.js` and then `app.js` on every page, and a reader who arrived
  at Ante-Nicene Fathers volume III from a search result pays ~101 KB gzipped of application before
  the text is interactive. The review asked for the shell to be dropped outright on corpus pages,
  "by class of page rather than by query string". Half of that is right and half is not, and the
  split makes the right half sharper: the median reading page is now 14 KB of HTML, so the shell is
  five times the page it rides on. But the shell IS this site's navigation on a phone — the six
  tabs, the soft navigation between them, the installed face — and dropping it by class of page
  would make the app appear and disappear depending on how a reader arrived: a tap from the tab bar
  into a library page is a soft navigation and keeps the shell, while a reload or a shared link on
  the same page would have none, and the tab bar would vanish mid-session. So the shell is LATE on a
  reading page rather than absent. `chrome.js` (the two fixed bars, ~22 KB with its chunk) still
  goes at once, so the first paint is still the app; `app.js` is appended once the page has loaded
  and the main thread is idle — or at the first `pointerdown` or keypress, whichever comes first, so
  a reader who reaches for a tab never waits on an idle callback a busy page may be slow to give.
  Everywhere else — the app pages, the hand pages, the home launcher — nothing changed: those pages
  ARE the app. The test is not a source-text lock: `tests/js/nav_shell.test.mjs` RUNS `docs/nav.js`
  in a stub DOM and asks what it appended and when, which is also how the meter's two gates are held
  (the beacon on the live hostname, never from `127.0.0.1` and never under `navigator.webdriver`).

## Build system

- **The runner's pandoc calls the footnotes something else, and the split shipped 358,049 dead marks
  to the link checker (2026-09-18).** The first CI run of the volume split failed at `make check`
  with a third of a million missing anchors — every footnote mark on every part page. Locally the
  same tree had link-checked clean twice. The difference was one tag: the dev box's pandoc closes a
  document with `<section id="footnotes" class="footnotes footnotes-end-of-document">`, and the
  runner's (newer) writes `<aside id="footnotes" …>` — confirmed by fetching a live corpus page and
  looking, rather than by guessing. The splitter's pattern knew only `<section>`, so on the runner
  it found no footnotes block at all, moved no notes into any part, and left every `href="#fnNNN"`
  pointing at a list that had stayed behind. Nothing about the pages looked wrong; they simply did
  not work. The pattern now matches `section`, `aside` or `div` by either its `id="footnotes"` or a
  `footnotes` class, and RE-EMITS the volume's own opening tag so a part looks exactly like the
  volume it came from. The second half of the fix is the one that matters more: **every part is now
  asked, before it is written, whether it can answer its own references** (`unresolved()` — every
  `href="#id"` must find an `id` on that page), and a volume with a part that cannot is left WHOLE,
  with a line saying which page and which anchor. An oversized page is a disappointment; a page of
  dead links is a lie. The same check found a second case in passing: an id that stays in the front
  matter (which does not travel) must be rewritten to the INDEX from a part, not left as a bare
  fragment. A live page from production, in the runner's own dialect, is now part of how this is
  verified — `Page` parses it, the split makes 119 parts of it, and the part citing note 1873 holds
  note 1873.
